Additionally, Identity Endorsements using the Security Protocol and Data Model (SPDM) are incorporated to ensure robust device identity management for Tenants and Cloud Service Providers (CSP).Furthermore, the updates include recommendations for binding evidence from the TEE Security Manager (TSM) and Device Security Manager (DSM) within TEE Device Interface Security Protocol (TDISP) configurations. These guidelines address vulnerabilities such as Man-in-the-Middle (MiM) and replay attacks, ensuring evidence from TSM and DSM are securely linked to maintain trusted relationships between components.

### Orchestrating Confidential Compute using OCP Secure Boot, Attestation and CXL IDE, TSP, DMTF SPDM Specs
Speakers: Sakul Gupta (Micron Technology Inc.)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7547/
Host-managed device memory (HDM) is device-attached memory mapped to system coherent address space and accessible to host using standard write-back semantics. Type 3 devices use OCP Secure boot, OCP attestation and CXL IDE in containment mode to protect the data in transit across exposed links. Device configures the IDE encryption keys using SPDM secured messages and records the public key hash of the device ID cert that the TSM authenticates to establish trust. TVM (Trusted Execution Environment Virtual Machine) is used to execute confidential compute workloads where both data-at-rest as well as data-in-motion are protected against unauthorized usage. A use-case using HIPPA workload for CC will be walked through link-IDE, Memory Encryption and establishment of secure channel between a TVM and the CXL-Type2/Type3 device leveraging industry standard practices of DMTF SPDM. Also, will articulate requirements of a “secure” CXL-switching fabric between the Host & a CXL-device(s).

### Porting to Caliptra Subsystem using MCU Firmware SDK
Speakers: Christopher Swenson (Microsoft), Xiling Sun (Microsoft)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7443/ | materials: https://github.com/chipsalliance/caliptra-mcu-sw
The Caliptra 2.0 core is an open-source root of trust (RoT) that can be utilized as part of a larger security subsystem. While the Caliptra core is a fixed IP with standard ROM and firmware, the subsystem is designed as a toolkit for that builds on the core IP with a mix of standard and customizable components. In this talk, we'll show how integrators can use our Rust-based firmware development kit, which provides common components to ease integrating with the Caliptra subsystem and building secure firmware. We'll discuss the design decisions that led to our project structure and demonstrate how to build a platform from scratch.

### Trust at 64 GT/s: Security Considerations for PCIe 6 Switch Deployments
Speakers: Avdhesh Chhodavdia (Astera Labs)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7479/
As the industry transitions from PCIe 5 to PCIe 6, maintaining end-to-end security is paramount! It requires a cohesive approach across devices, switches, and system architectures. This presentation will explore what must align for end-to-end security and Confidential Compute scenarios for secure PCIe 6 deployments. It will examine device-level features including secure boot and attestation to system-wide trust boundaries and policy enforcement. The presentation will also cover key challenges introduced by PCIe 6 with its higher speeds and PAM4 signaling, impacting signal integrity, side-channel exposure, and authentication mechanisms. Attendees will also gain insights into the hardware and firmware dependencies that must be coordinated to build a secure and scalable PCIe 6 infrastructure.

### Centralized System Security Hub for Server Platforms
Speakers: Phanikumar Kancharla (Marvell), Craig Barner (Marvell)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7456/ | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Dd9TloU4bgk | materials: https://drive.google.com/file/d/1R_6eaaGkspdFBpwbMrpbPzTmBJe1alF-/view?usp=drive_link
At OCP EMEA 2025, we introduced the concept of a Centralized System Security Hub to validate the need for secure, hardware-level key distribution beyond storage. The strong community interest confirmed the urgency of addressing this challenge across DRAM, PCIe, CXL, and accelerators. In this session, we present the high-level design of a secure hardware channel and protocol for key provisioning, along with software integration interfaces that enable seamless adoption across platforms. The design supports seamless integration with system software and complements Caliptra and LOCK, or can function independently as a scalable security framework for cloud and virtualized environments.

## Server: AI HW SW Co-Design/NIC/HPC

### Enabling macroheterogeneity through a “system-of-systems” approach
Speakers: Samantika Sury (Hewlett Packard Enterprise), Tushar Krishna (Georgia Institute of Technology)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7429/ | materials: https://www.linkedin.com/in/samantika/
Systems are often homogenous for usability and scalability. However enabling macroheterogeneity can be valuable for AI co-design. Diverse phases can potentially map well to different compute partitions such as modeling/simulation partition, inference partition and data analysis partition. AI surrogates can also replace parts of an application to support inference queries. A system of systems can perform well if there is a common framework for fabrics, data services, resource scheduling, runtimes, and system management to sustain high utilization. The framework, which includes both hardware elements and software APIs and tools also needs to be flexible enough to both loosely and tightly integrate the partitions based on communication requirements. This talk will discuss the use cases for a system of systems, architectural challenges and tools available to address some of these challenges, and present the performance potential using relevant applications.

### Coordination and workload algorithms to unlock the potential of polymorphic ‘System-of-Systems’ architectures for large scale AI
Speakers: Jascha Achterberg (University of Oxford), Kingshuk Mandal (Keysight Technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7277/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/documents/ocp-document-submission-ai-co-design-docx-pdf | pdf: https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.02813
The AI landscape is rapidly evolving into complex "systems of systems" where disaggregated, composable, and heterogeneous resources are orchestrated to meet the demands of large-scale AI workloads. To truly unlock the potential of AI on polymorphic compute stacks with diverse accelerators, fabrics, and storage, we need to address two questions: How are existing workloads best coordinated across heterogeneous architectures? Which new workloads become scalable through heterogeneous compute? This talk presents the AI HW-SW CoDesign workstream's algorithmic solutions: graph sharding algorithms that interact with emulation software to predict optimal deployment strategies for established workloads, alongside algorithmic priors that transform workloads into more efficiently distributable compute graphs. Together, these algorithms enable AI workloads to co-evolve with emerging heterogeneous hardware stacks, resulting in new AI systems which would not be scalable on current technology.

### Community Panel - Enabling Immersion by Design: Aligning Server and Cooling Communities
Speakers: John Stuewe (OCP), Austin Hipes (UNICOM Engineering), Samantha Yates (Intel Corporation), Matt Bowman (Meta)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6860/
Immersion cooling imposes unique demands on server design, from material compatibility to mechanical layout, connector resilience, and long-term reliability. This panel brings together leaders from the Server Project and the Immersion Project to explore how the OCP community can accelerate IT equipment readiness for immersion environments. With insights from the Immersion Technical Committee chairs and both Server and Immersion Project leaders, the discussion will cover practical design considerations, gaps in standardization, qualification pathways, and how deeper cross-project collaboration can pave the way for truly immersion-optimized servers.

### Memory technology optimized for at-scale AI systems: Bandwidth, Capacity, and Connectivity
Speakers: Siamak Tavallaei (Samsung), Millind Mittal (MemWize)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7337/
As data-intensive applications continue to scale, throughput-computing workloads demand efficient data-movers. High-speed interconnects such as UCIe are trending to become standard for die-to-die interconnect; while photonics will play a significant role for high-speed chip-to-chip interconnect. Within memory-optimized architectures, capacity and bandwidth considerations have their own places within an at-scale system; however, for all-to-all data-exchange, we will also need efficient, unimpeded connectivity between peer devices while maintaining physical serviceability. In this presentation, we explore a memory-oriented, high-speed interconnect starting from an internal, wide d2d interconnect toward and external, serial interconnect such as photonics for at-scale AI/ML systems.

### Power Conversion Solutions for future Server Boards operating directly from HV DC
Speakers: Gerald Deboy (Infineon Technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7205/
With ever higher power consumption of modern GPUs and the need to cluster many more GPUs per rack, rack power levels will soon scale up to +1 MW. These power levels mandate a change in architecture towards HV DC, replacing the well-established 48V ecosystem. Future Server boards will operate directly from 800V or +/- 400V respectively. This requires new functions such as eFuse to pre-charge server boards before plugging to the HV DC bus and discharging switches to avoid any hazardous voltages in case of removing a server board from the IT rack. Power needs to be converted from HV DC down to the GPU supply voltages of less than 1V. We are investigating both 800V-to-50V and 800V-to-12V as essential links to well established low voltage domains for Server boards today. The presentation will show possible solutions and give KPIs such as power density and measured efficiency data. The designs are running on very high switching frequency close to 1 MHz and draw full benefit from GaN HEMTs.

### Scaling OCP NIC to 1.6T and beyond for AI
Speakers: Hemal Shah (Broadcom), Damien Chong (Meta)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6945/
Artificial Intelligence (AI) Training applications consume massive computing resources a c ross large server clusters. Scaling servers within a rack is increasingly difficult and is bounded by mechanical, thermal and system management challenges. Scaling beyond a rack requires support for higher Scale-Out bandwidth while requiring efficient space and power utilization. We will discuss the current state of the NIC form factors, including its top speed, size, manageability and challenges. We will then examine key technical challenges to be addressed to achieve higher speeds to 1.6T, 3.2T and beyond for AI networks.

### Powering the Future of AI: High Power Density Solutions for AI Accelerators
Speakers: Surendhar Somasundaram (Infineon Technologies), Paul Yeaman (Infineon Technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7552/ | materials: https://www.bodospower.com/pdf/bp_2024_01.pdf | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RzIaF7ZO0X4&t=1156s
The rapid adoption of AI and machine learning has dramatically increased demand for compute power, driven by high-performance xPUs and AI accelerators. This growth follows a "new Moore’s Law," where compute capability scales with power consumption. Modern GPUs exceed 1000A at sub-0.8V, with projections surpassing 4000A within 3-5 years, pushing existing power architectures to their limits. This presentation introduces high power density VRM solutions leveraging Vertical Power Delivery (VPD) to meet the demands of AI accelerators. VPD enables high current density, reduced PDN losses, and improved system efficiency. However, its adoption presents challenges, such as limited space beneath processors, thermal management, and supporting high currents while maintaining low height profiles. We will discuss key design principles, showcase a 1500A VPD hardware platform, and highlight its potential impact on next-generation AI accelerators.

### PCIe 7.0 Enhancements for high-speed I/O connectivity for OCP server Infrastructure
Speakers: Hemal Shah (Broadcom), Travis Hamilton (Arm)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7236/
To meet the demands of AI, PCIe 7.0 compliant servers may be needed sooner than anticipated. Advanced features including load balancing, Unordered I/O (UIO), and Virtual Channels (VCs) become essential for achieving high-bandwidth PCIe 7.0 connectivity between accelerators and NICs. In this talk, we will discuss these advanced features that need to be considered for the next generation servers, PCIe switches, accelerators, and NICs as well as showing performance modeling results of the benefits of these features. We will also describe the implications of these features on the design of OCP servers, OAMs, and OCP NICs.

### OCP NIC 2025: Now and Beyond
Speakers: Thomas Ng (Intel Corporation), Jason Rock (Dell Technologies), Joseph Hanson Vazquez (Intel), Satyam Saini (Meta)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6842/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/w/index.php?title=Server/NIC
Unlock the future of AI and HPC with OCP NIC! Supporting up to 32 lanes of PCIe Gen 6, and 800 Gbps+ per port, OCP NIC is designed for interoperable high-performance and scalable solutions across diverse environments for modern computing. The form-factor continues to advance with PCIe Gen 7 and 1.6 Tbps I/O on the horizon. Join us for a discussion of what’s in store for OCP NIC, its associated challenges, and how we anticipate future workloads and cooling requirements.

## Server: Composable Memory Systems

### OCP Composable Memory Systems (CMS)  Sub-Project Technical Update
Speakers: Manoj Wadekar (Meta), Anjaneya "Reddy" Chagam (Intel)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7246/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/wiki/Server/CMS
The OCP Composable Memory Systems (CMS) sub-project is developing scalable memory architectures and hardware/software co-designs to address cloud service providers’ needs for memory expansion, pooling, and sharing - critical for both general-purpose computing and AI workloads. This session will provide an update on the CMS Sub-Project goals, highlight progress over the past year, and outline next steps toward enabling efficient, disaggregated memory solutions within the OCP ecosystem.

### Composable Memory Fabric Architecture: Enabling Scalable Memory Management for AI and General Purpose Compute
Speakers: Hongjian Fan (Seagate), Anjaneya "Reddy" Chagam (Intel)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7249/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/wiki/Server/CMS
The rising demands of workloads such as in-memory databases, caching, and AI are driving the need for scalable memory expansion, pooling, and sharing solutions. Cache-coherent interconnects like Compute Express Link (CXL) provide mechanisms to dynamically provision memory resources tailored to workload requirements. The OCP CMS "Memory Fabric Orchestration Workstream" is dedicated to developing a composable memory orchestration architecture by collaborating with standards organizations, enabling open and interoperable solutions, and supporting ecosystem vendor reference implementations to accelerate broad market adoption. This technical talk presents an in-depth overview of the memory fabric architecture, which leverages CXL and DMTF interfaces to empower data center orchestration tools, such as Kubernetes, to efficiently manage memory resources across the data center. The talk will also highlight future focus areas for the CMS Memory Fabric Orchestration Workstream .

### DC Memory Fabric management: An In-Depth Review of the K8s OCP Reference Implementation
Speakers: Grant Mackey (Jackrabbit Labs), Adam Manzanares (Samsung)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7039/ | materials: https://blog.jrlabs.io/posts/2024-10-25-jrl-ocp-presentation/ | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=biv7R8W5Zhs
The CMS fabric workstream has published a white paper for Memory Fabric Orchestration Architectures. An entity we call the Data Center Memory Fabric Manager (DCMFM) is the control and flow touch point for far memory devices and fabrics like Compute Express Link (CXL). The application of the concepts, roles and responsibilities presented in the white paper are not straight-forward at this time as implementation examples are nascent. Last year CMS participants demonstrated how Kubernetes can transparently utilize CXL-attached memory for end-user workloads. In a follow on presentation, we revisit the implementation with formalized roles and responsibilities of the various components in the end-to-end system. These key details are documented in the DCMFM fabric white paper, but this presentation gives attendees the opportunity to learn how terminology from the white paper is applied to a real world implementation of a data center resource orchestrator and CXL-attached memory devices.

### End-to-End Management of a CXL-enabled Datacenter Memory Fabric
Speakers: Sumit Puri (Liqid), Brian Pan (H3 Platform), Han Deok Lee (Samsung), Taehoon Lee (RedHat)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7300/
As data-intensive applications continue to scale, traditional server architectures are increasingly constrained by fixed memory configurations and limited bandwidth. Compute Express Link (CXL®) introduces a transformative solution: a dynamic, fabric-based memory architecture that decouples memory from compute, enabling greater flexibility, scalability, and efficiency across the datacenter. In this presentation, we will explore the end-to-end management of a CXL-based datacenter memory fabric. The session will cover key architectural elements such as CXL-attached memory modules and switching infrastructure, composable software orchestration, and real-world deployment strategies. The speakers will demonstrate how the integration of advanced CXL memory, high-performance CXL switches, and fabric management & orchestration software enables a fully dynamic, software-defined memory pool that can be provisioned and scaled in real time.

### Emerging Software Patterns for Programming New Memory Devices and Interfaces
Speakers: Pankaj Mehra (Elephance Memory), Gaurav Agarwal (Marvell)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7026/ | materials: https://ocp-all.groups.io/g/OCP-CMS-Computational-Programming/files/OCP%20CMS%20CxP%20Principles%20and%20Practices%20Whitepaper
Beyond maturing software for North-South data movement in memory hierarchy with expanders and pools, programming East-West communication among cooperating accelerators and computational memory devices still needs new enablement and optimizations to exploit CXL's new communication patterns. For this, we present the guiding principles and best practices documented by the Server/CMS/CxP workstream in our whitepaper. Emerging fabric-attached, shared, disaggregated, and nonvolatile memories continue to open new challenges and opportunities for software work. We present the requirements for developing stable hardware-software interfaces in this rapidly evolving field and posit an architecture direction aligned with the broader ecosystem of open-source stacks, open data formats, and related standards, thus identifying ways to enable adoption of new memory devices and interfaces for improved power and performance while overcoming known impediments to broad, long-term adoption.

### Journey of a Secure-boot fault, logged and signaled as an Error, from ROM to the host, for RAS
Speakers: Sakul Gupta (Micron Technology Inc.), Manjunaatha Harapanahalli (Intel Corporation)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7305/
The CXL memory modules plugged into OCP servers go through OCP secure boot and measured boot, compliant with the OCP Secure boot and recovery specs. The device's Secure enclave's ROM logs any error to event queues in SRAM and then mutable FW logs them to SPI NOR and reports to the host in a standardized log format like CPER, for Firmware First or BMC managed error handling or RAS action. Based on CSP agreed upon policy, aligned with the OCP Fault management specs, or OCP FM RAS API specs, a certain type of signaling mechanism is raised for critical errors, to notify the host and to initiate RAS actions. CXL devices can autonomously initiate recovery actions or instantiate side-band interfaces to start host-orchestrated recovery. Host can be read logs via CXL spec defined CCI commands such as Get_Log and also publish via BDAT table which is available for the BMC or the OS to consume. Further action can be conveyed to the host using CPAD format defined by OCP HWFM RAS API workstream.

### Benchmarking is Hard: A ‘Hermetically Sealed’ Container for CMS Workload Benchmarking
Speakers: Grant Mackey (Jackrabbit Labs), Vikrant Soman (Uber)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7022/ | materials: https://github.com/opencomputeproject/OCP-SVR-CMS-Benchmarks
The CMS GitHub hosts relevant industry benchmarks for technology like CXL. However, ensuring fair/repeatable workload deployment and results has hindered adoption. We introduce an open-source, "hermetically sealed" container to simplify benchmarking for vendors and end-users, fostering a rich source of performance data for TCO and ROI analysis. A "look but don't touch" approach, the container has all necessary software and dependencies, making benchmarks deployable across various operating systems and hardware platforms with minimal user support. This runtime standardization ensures repeatable, comparable results. The containers have configurable input parameters and standardized output, allowing for confident comparison across different system configurations. We demonstrate the initial workload container, STREAM and MLC, across diverse CXL memory devices from various vendors and systems. We also guide attendees on parsing and analyzing the resulting JSON for fair system comparisons.

### Evaluating CXL-Based Composable Memory Solutions: A TCO Framework for Datacenter Workloads
Speakers: Navneet Rao (Altera), Nilesh Shah (zeropoint technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6947/
The OCP CMS group has conducted evaluations of datacenter workloads: content caching, AI/data analytics, legacy applications, demonstrated potential benefits of composable CXL. This presentation covers a TCO framework designed to assist end users and datacenter operators in assessing the ROI of integrating CMS solutions into their infrastructure: CMS Native Expansion: Memory expansion TCO for the provisioning of discrete memory technologies-- Bandwidth Expansion: Comparative analysis of DDR4/DDR5 versus DDR4/5 + CXL for SPARK workloads. Capacity Expansion: Examination of single NUMA node configurations with CXL, DRAM, and CXL+DRAM interleaving as well as Thin Memory provisioning/ compression CMS Pooled Memory Expansion: Analysis of CXL-attached memory usage for Capacity Expansion : Capability: Evaluation of co-located storage workloads, such as Cassandra, Redis, and MySQL, Efficiency: Mitigation of memory stranding through the utilization of pooled memory.

### Co-Designing for Scale: CXL-Based Memory Solution for Data-Centric Workloads
Speakers: Gaurav Agarwal (Marvell), Anil Godbole (Intel), Jianping Jiang (Xconn Technologies Holdings), Xinjun Yang (Alibaba)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7396/ | materials: https://github.com/ai-dynamo/dynamo | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=2HlzeYXET8w
This talk explores hardware-software co-optimized solutions for memory-bound workloads by leveraging disaggregated and composable CXL resources to augment memory-constrained AI accelerators. We present flexible system architectures tailored for data-intensive workloads—such as billion-scale vector search, cloud-native and in-memory databases, data analytics, and large-scale AI inferencing. We highlight innovative approaches to improve efficiency, reduce TCO, and enhance system performance by utilizing cutting-edge, CXL-based composable memory systems. These include switch-based disaggregated memory, Near Memory Compute accelerators, and memory pooling appliances. To address software challenges introduced by heterogeneity, we leverage advanced open-source frameworks such as NVIDIA Dynamo, along with KV-cache-focused solutions like Mooncake and LMCache. These innovations help lower adoption barriers and pave the way for cost-effective, scalable, and energy-efficient AI infrastructure.

### Large CXL Memory Expansion Systems and Workloads
Speakers: Sumit Puri (Liqid), Ajay Joshi (Micron)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7446/ | materials: https://computeexpresslink.org/ | video: https://youtu.be/gyqpH8UVXkk?si=nfWAVHmayzVGcFwX
This presentation will introduce the architecture of a large-scale CXL memory expansion system, purpose-built to deliver breakthrough performance for 100 TiB-class workloads and shared memory compute paradigms. CXL Consortium members Liqid and Micron will discuss how they are deploying novel CXL-based system architectures to maximize performance and capacity for demanding applications: • Dramatically increasing size limits as to “what fits in memory” • Composable provisioning of large amounts of memory for both memory-expansion (pooling) and shared access • Enabling existing apps and work flows to achieve memory de-duplication through shared memory, enabled by the open-source fabric-attached memory file system (famfs) • Enabling data scaling without sharding for workloads that can’t be sharded or quantized effectively

### Multi-Headed Composable Memory Appliance (MHCMA) Next-Gen
Speakers: Mohamad El-Batal (Seagate), Hongjian Fan (Seagate)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7532/
The initial FPGA based contributed CMA was capable of up to 2 x16 PCIe-Gen5 CXL1.1 Host ports per Blade and a total of 8x Blades per 19" shelf CXL Fabric Managed Composable Pooled Memory Appliance. The next stage is to define the architecrture of the 2nd generation MHCMA using a next generation 12nm ASIC device with up to 8 x8 PCIe-Gen5 CXL2.0 Host Ports per blade with up to 8x Blades per 21" OCP Pooled and/or Shared CXL Fabric Managed MHCMA Chassis architecture. We want to have a session to show our current POR and get feedback from key interested parties in joining this effort under CLA.

### Latest Academic Research for Composable Memory
Speakers: Jongse Park (KAIST), Antonis Psistakis (University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7521/ | materials: https://jongse-park.github.io/
The Composable Memory Systems subproject’s Academic Research Workstream brings cutting-edge academic researchers to present their research on advanced CXL use cases and characterization of composable memory systems to the CMS industry partners. In this panel session, two academic researchers who have published their CMS-related research, in top academic will give lightning presentations on their research into new ways to use CXL devices and disaggregated memory systems. They will also participate in a moderated panel discussion to highlight their future areas of research, to outline how their research program aligns with industry’s needs, and to provide insights on how increased interactions with industry leads to more impactful research.

### Accelerating Real-World CXL Deployments: From Hardware-Assisted Intelligent Tiering to Compressed Memory Prototypes
Speakers: Jungmin Choi (SK hynix), Dimitrios Palyvos (zeropoint technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6831/
This session presents two practical innovations advancing real-world CXL memory adoption. First, we introduce the CXL Hot-range Monitoring Unit (CHMU), an in-device hardware engine that detects hot memory regions in real-time. Integrated into an FPGA-based prototype, CHMU enables intelligent tiering decisions such as page migration, achieving up to 40% performance gains on AI workloads like DLRM compared to CXL-only configurations. Next, we unveil a full-stack compressed memory system aligned with the OCP Hyperscale Tiered Memory Expander Spec. Our system supports cacheline-level compression and runs real CMS workloads (e.g., Hyrise, HPCG) under MGLRU-based tiering. This platform informs open TCO models and demonstrates tangible gains in $/GB and server efficiency. Together, these efforts translate CXL specs into deployable systems, offering actionable insights for building dynamic, cost-effective tiered memory solutions at scale.

### Interconnects and Connectivity for At-scale AI: Shaping the Future of Interconnects and Memory Fabric for Open AI Systems
Speakers: Samir Rajadnya (Microsoft), Manoj Wadekar (Meta), Durgesh Srivastava (DataraAI), Brian Hirano (XCENA)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7313/
As data-intensive applications continue to scale, throughput-computing workloads demand a fresh look. In response, the computer industry is working on various memory fabric-oriented proposals such as CXL®, UALink, NVLink, and SUE for efficient data-movement in scale-up systems between xPUs, memory, and other end-devices. In this presentation, the panelists explore the latest trends in high-speed interconnect starting from an internal, d2d interconnect such as UCIe toward and external interconnect such as photonics for at-scale AI/ML systems. They will discuss technical and business aspects of the trade-offs they are making for scale-up and scale-out systems. As part of the OCP/CMS effort, the panelists will present their insights into how composable systems may help increase system efficiency to drive added value via disaggregated compute, memory, and storage elements.

## Server: MHS

### Advancing DC-MHS: Enabling Next-Gen Architecture and AI with Ecosystem Continuity
Speakers: Michael Gregoire (Dell Technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6871/
This year, the DC-MHS team has focused on expanding support for emerging architectures to support the AI revolution while preserving the ecosystem compatibility established in previous specification releases. Key initiatives include: Defining the architecture for native 48V HPMs, Supporting the new, more flexible Compact Form Factor (CFF) DC-SCM module, and Introducing a new M-SDNO HPM class that enables direct docking of M-CRPS PSUs while maintaining chassis compatibility with existing HPMs. In this panel, the team will share the goals, design approach, and practical use cases behind each of these architectural advancements.

### Yosemite v5, Meta’s next generation modular compute and storage platform
Speakers: Abe Garcia (Meta)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6881/
To better meet the Data Center infrastructure, use case needs and module reuse, the Yosemite v5 single socket server platform was designed ground up to support high power CPU, evolving DDR, LPDDR and CXL memory while leveraging the OCP DC-MHS SDNO HPM and DC-SCM. This talk will go over the new Yosemite v5 platform architecture, how we enabled the new features, while retaining the modular and shared hardware for better TCO and sustainability. The challenges we faced along the way and how we overcame them will also be touched.

### DC-MHS: Enabling 48V architecture and connectivity in the MHS ecosystem.
Speakers: Hal Loket (TE Connectivity)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7348/
The shift to 48V architecture within DC-MHS addresses growing AI/ML power demands in data centers. This transition offers improved efficiency and higher power delivery capabilities. New connectivity is key to this architecture, and DC-MHS worked diligently with community and interconnect experts to identify the best open solution. The 48V PICPWR & Dedicated Supply connectors enable the safe and efficient 48V power distribution from the busbar throughout MHS server systems, supporting the transition from traditional 12V architectures. By leveraging these DC-MHS connectors and updated specifications, 48V architectures can deliver the increased power levels required for next-generation servers and data center operations.

### MHS DC-SCM 2.x and 3.0 Workstreams - Current Developments and What’s Ahead
Speakers: Tim Lambert (Dell Technologies, Inc.), Javier Lasa (Intel)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7154/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/w/index.php?title=Server/MHS/DC-SCM-Specs-and-Designs
DC-SCM has become a foundational element in modern server designs, with widespread adoption across Cloud, Enterprise, AI, and Edge platforms. At last year’s OCP Summit, it was rare to find a system that didn’t support the DC-SCM 2.0 module, highlighting strong industry momentum. The DC-SCM 2.2 update marks the final major revision of the 2.x series and reflects the specification’s maturity. It introduces key enhancements, including a new Compact Form Factor (CFF) tailored to AI and Edge needs. With the upcoming release of the Type 2 HCC Design Spec, the 2.x portfolio will be functionally complete. Meanwhile, DC-SCM 3.0 development is underway, focused on simplification, optimization, and scalability to support Multi-HPM and Multi-Node architectures. This session will provide a concise overview of DC-SCM 2.2 and share the current status, design directions, and architectural goals of the evolving DC-SCM 3.0 spec.

### MHS: Modular Plug-and-Play (M-PNP) Workstream Updates and Key Topics Deep Dive
Speakers: Phillip Leech (HPE), Jeff Kennedy (Dell Technologies), Todd Rosedahl (Jabil)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7247/
The MHS Plug-and-Play (M-PNP) FPGA specification addresses the challenges of ensuring Secure Control Module (SCM), Host Processor Module (HPM) and peripheral interoperability by specifying buses, interfaces, protocol layers, APIs, and data models for BMC consumption. This specification works in conjunction with other MHS specifications to provide consistent management interfaces and system behaviors that facilitate rapid platform enablement and greater development efficiency. The MPNP.FPGA specification outlines several key sections that support the M-PNP framework. Key sections include: Standardized IP blocks with common CSRs and memory map Standard interfaces (e.g. LTPI, SGPIO, M-PESTI, etc.) FlexIO / Sideband signal configuration Standardized MUX control Virtual wire framework FPGA JSON self-descriptive data for BMC consumption We will share significant progress and updates captured in the M-PNP.FPGA 0.5 specification release as well as plans for future work.

## Server: Open Chiplet Economy

### Open Chiplet Economy's attempt to address the 3rd party chiplet interoperability at a System level
Speakers: Anu Ramamurthy (Microchip), Jawad Nasrullah (Palo Alto Electron)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7419/
We want to bring attention to interoperability in systems that use chiplets from multiple vendors. The focus moves from D2D PHY to the higher layers of the stack including firmware layers. We also introduce system level virtual prototyping as a way to determining interoperability at a early stage to improve efficiency. Given how important these are for the Chiplet Marketplace to thrive, we at the OCE have started new workstreams to engage in this activity and provide guidelines that will help to increase the possibility of interop.

### Building an Interoperable Chiplet Ecosystem through Golden Die Validation with Pre-Silicon Correlation
Speakers: Michael Klempa (Alphawave Semi), Pedro Merlo (Keysight Technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6924/ | materials: https://awavesemi.com/press-release/alphawave-semi-tapes-out-industry-first-multi-protocol-i-o-connectivity-chiplet-for-high-performance-compute-and-ai-infrastructure/
As AI, cloud, and HPC drive demand for disaggregated architectures, a robust, interoperable chiplet ecosystem is essential. This presentation introduces a framework centered on the UCIe standard and a novel “golden die” methodology to validate chiplet interoperability. With no open-source golden die available, we demonstrate how existing UCIe IP can serve as a reference, using BIST and RAS features calibrated against known signals to verify link integrity. This enables compliance testing and supports scalable, multi-vendor integration. The framework also addresses a path towards compliance for beyond-224G connectivity leveraging D2D links. A detailed workflow is presented for characterizing TX, interconnect, and RX using calibrated test points and stress conditions, with results correlated to pre-silicon EDA simulations. This approach accelerates design cycles and enhances system reliability, paving the way for an open chiplet economy.

### Breaking real world barriers for Open Chiplet Adoption
Speakers: Kash Johal (YorChip)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7364/
There is a concerted effort at OCP to breakthrough the barriers to an Open Chiplet Economy – from security, KGD/Testability, Chiplet System Manageability and Business group: Security: Discuss Caliptra and OCP effort – and ask what it takes to build a secure boot chiplet for Mass Adoption. KGD/Testability: Discuss current approaches and note new developments for BIST in Die and Packaged form to assign responsibility for bad die. Chiplet System Manageability – new group starting solving issues for Open Chiplet economy. Chiplet Business Group: Addressing efforts to ensure compliance and standardize on model availability for seamless adoption.

### BoW 2.1 Enhancements for New Applications
Speakers: Kevin Donnelly (Eliyan), Morgan Whately (Infineon Technologies)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6872/ | materials: https://drive.google.com/drive/folders/1F90EXML8v6rB3MJXaEIPCPCBIqCqsOAt
During the last two decades, increases in compute performance in FLOPs has far outpaced the increases in memory bandwidth, leading to limitations in system performance often described as the Memory Wall. Many designers are considering die-to-die (D2D) interfaces like Bunch of Wires (BoW) to improve performance in ASIC-to-memory connections. This talk will review the BoW Memory Addendum to the BoW PHY 2.0 Specification, a new OCP contribution that is expected to be published before OCP Global Summit 2025. The BoW Memory Addendum describes optional enhancements that enable the BoW D2D interface to more efficiently connect ASICs to memory device chiplets, providing improved performance at lower power than traditional off-package memory interfaces.

### Chiplets with eFlash IP
Speakers: Feng Zhou (Microchip)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7712/
Chiplets with eFlash IP

### Universal Die-to-Die Chiplet Interfacing Standards – The Road to Interoperability within the OCP Chiplet Marketplace
Speakers: Jeff Maguire (Ventana Micro Systems)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7047/ | materials: https://github.com/opencomputeproject/ODSA-Link-Layer/blob/main/Specifications/ODSA%20Transaction%20and%20Link%20Layer%20Specification%20for%20BoW%20Interfaces%20Rev%20A%20-%20v0.9.0.pdf
While the semiconductor industry has recognized the benefits of chiplet integrations, multi-vendor chiplet interoperability remains the biggest challenge to widespread adoption. To address this challenge, Ventana contributed the “ODSA D2D Transaction and Link Layer (TLL) Specification for BoW Interfaces”. Recently UCIe has gained significant market traction, however protocol interoperability has been left out-of-scope aside from PCIe and CXL which are not relevant for ultra-low latency chiplet integration strategies. Ventana will introduce our latest OCP contribution on TTL binding to the UCIe Flit-Aware Die-to-Die Interface (FDI). In addition, a wide range of protocol mappings, namely AMBA protocols, to the TLL interfacing layer will be discussed with a call to action to help define and refine mappings. The aim is to create a vast set of interoperable chiplets within the OCP Chiplet Marketplace which can address a wide range of applications and market segment needs.

### UCIe-Based Chiplet Ecosystem: Interoperable Testbench for Multi-Vendor IP Integration
Speakers: Luis E. Rodriguez (Siemens), Prashant Dixit (Siemens)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6888/
The chiplet ecosystem and standards are still in their infancy. Consequently, official interoperability and compliance programs from groups like the UCIe Working Group are not yet available. It usually takes a few years after a spec is published for such programs to begin, since silicon isn’t available immediately. This creates risk for early adopters but also opens opportunities for early design collaboration. We describe a simulation-based interoperability program that integrates UCIe IP from two companies into a simulation environment, focusing on LogPhy and D2D interoperability in two stages. Verification IP for UCIe is used to create test cases and drive traffic through RDI and FDI interfaces. We discuss the challenges during the interoperability effort, test cases created, and results. We also explore how other companies can join similar efforts to accelerate UCIe adoption and rollout.

### Rearchitecting AI Infrastructure with General-Purpose and Accelerator Chiplets
Speakers: Santhosh Srinath (Meta), Jinwook Oh (Rebellions), Daniel Ping (Novatek)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7326/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/chiplets/68/arm-chiplet-system-architecture
As AI workloads rapidly evolve- the industry is moving toward modular- chiplet-based architectures that combine general-purpose compute and domain-specific acceleration. This panel will bring together leaders from across the Arm ecosystem‚ Äîincluding silicon providers- hyperscalers- and EDA/toolchain partners‚Äîto discuss how Arm-based chiplets are enabling flexible- scalable- and energy-efficient solutions for AI and general-purpose infrastructure.\n\nPanelists will explore the technical and ecosystem innovations driving this transformation- including coherent interconnects like AMBA CHI C2C- open standards for interoperability- and advancements in lifecycle analytics- packaging- and system integration. The session will provide a forward-looking view into how Arm‚Äôs compute platforms are enabling a new generation of composable- AI-ready infrastructure tailored for the needs of hyperscale data centers and edge environments alike.

### OCE Transaction and Link Layer Specification 1.2
Speakers: Helia Naemi (Astera Labs), Brian Murray (VeriSilicon)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7812/
OCE Transaction and Link Layer Specification 1.2

### Accelerating Early-Stage Exploration with Virtual Prototyping for a Flourishing Multi-Vendor Chiplet Economy
Speakers: Raghu Shankar (Entrepreneur)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7015/ | materials: https://www.accellera.org/downloads/standards/systemc | pdf: https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/10246125
Diverse multi-vendor designs will drive large system-in-packages (SIPs) to effectively meet diverse market needs. Developing a robust ecosystem of architecturally compatible designs early in the planning and prototyping phase enables agile, high-confidence design space exploration, thereby fostering a thriving market economy downstream. This necessitates collaboration among chip, chiplet, EDA, and IP vendors in an open ecosystem during the early development stages. By working together, vendors can innovate and differentiate in specialized domains while driving towards profitability and architectural compatibility. The proposed framework (complementary to chiplet profiling) speeds up early-stage decision-making with minimal risk. It facilitates identification of architectural compatible choices, automation and scaling of assessments, along with business and technical value estimates. This drives market growth, accelerates time-to-revenue, ensures better product-market fit and lower NRE

### Roofline analysis and profiling of HPC applications to guide system design
Speakers: George Michelogiannakis (Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6800/ | pdf: https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1109/SC41406.2024.00062 | materials: https://crd.lbl.gov/assets/Uploads/PMBS24_DCGM_final.pdf
Performance profiles of HPC and AI scientific kernels range widely from memory intensive to compute intensive. Roofline charts and other key performance compute, memory, and communication metrics are useful to characterize and guide performance tuning of applications onto a particular hardware, as well as improve system designs of next-gen offerings. We will present recent application profile results and the collection method that HPC & AI modularity OCP workstream members collected or generated from production HPC clusters like Perlmutter for key scientific HPC and AI applications. We will then discuss how these results can be used to design and configure specialized chiplets, and integrate them in the same package in a modular fashion in order to maximize application efficiency.

### CDX Innovations in Chiplet Design: From Standardization to System Realization
Speakers: Seungmin Woo (Georgia Institute of Technology), David Ratchkov (Anemoi Software Inc), Jawad Nasrullah (Palo Alto Electron)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7083/
This presentation highlights CDX’s ongoing contributions to advancing chiplet design through standardization and demonstrative implementation. Key updates to the CDXML specification include support for Assembly Design Kits (ADK), Material Design Kits (MDK), and Test Design Kits (TDK), enabling a more complete digital twin framework for chiplet-based systems. We will also share progress on the CDX demonstration design, which showcases a high-performance architecture integrating HBM and UCIe-connected chiplets on a glass interposer. This work aims to validate early planning, co-design, and system integration workflows essential for next-generation heterogeneous packaging.

### Introducing the OCP Chiplet Economy Chiplet Systems Workstream: A Call for Multi-Vendor Interoperability
Speakers: Itai Nadler (GSOC LTD)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6993/ | materials: https://www.opencompute.org/w/index.php?title=Server/OpenChipletEconomy/ChipletSystems
The semiconductor industry's move to chiplet architectures is designed to lower costs and increase design flexibility but encounters multi-vendor integration challenges due to proprietary interfaces and incompatible specifications. The OCP Chiplet Economy Chiplet Systems workstream aims to address these issues by establishing standards for cross-supplier integration, leading to cost savings and quicker time-to-market. This workstream targets six technical areas: · Silicon and Systems Architecture standardization · Security frameworks for inter-vendor trust · Power efficiency protocols · Performance optimization methodologies · Compatibility and validation frameworks · PHY and link alignment specifications This presentation will detail the workstream's scope, structure, participation model, and approach to standards development, emphasizing the benefits of industry collaboration in fostering interoperable chiplet ecosystems.

### Accelerating Chiplet Integration Through ASE VIPack™  and Arm Total Design Collaboration
Speakers: Lihong Cao (ASE Group), Sridhar Valluru (Arm)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7447/
ASE is enabling scalable chiplet integration for AI and cloud systems through VIPack™ advanced packaging technology in collaboration with Arm Total Design (ATD) and the broader chiplet marketplace. VIPack™ platform featuring FOCoS, 2.5D/3D integration, and Co-Packaged Optics, supports high-density die-to-die connectivity and heterogeneous packaging aligned with OCP and UCIe interoperability goals. To streamline the development, ASE’s IDE 2.0 bridges the gap between silicon and system-level design, offering early co-design, electrical and thermal modeling, and automated verification to reduce risk and time. ASE and Arm propose a collaborative initiative with OCP and ATD stakeholders to define a reference chiplet design and packaging flow based on ecosystem standards. This includes substrate templates, signal integrity practices, and co-design checkpoints to simplify SoC integration and accelerate innovation for hyperscale, edge, and automotive applications.

### Smashing Memory & I/O Limits: Architectures for the GenAI Era
Speakers: Ramin Farjadrad (Eliyan)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7430/
GenAI training and inference workloads are growing at an unprecedented pace — yet today’s system architectures are increasingly limited by Memory and I/O bottlenecks. In this talk, Eliyan will present a set of innovative system-level architectures that break through these barriers: Custom HBM on FinFET base die to enable memory expansion and unprecedented bandwidth density, overcoming traditional HBM packaging constraints and breaking the "Memory Wall." D2D-to-C2C fanout architectures to dramatically improve XPU-XPU and system-level interconnect bandwidth, power efficiency, and scalability — addressing the "I/O Wall" and unlocking new possibilities for on-board and optical connectivity. These solutions result in highly efficient, manufacturable GenAI systems built with open, scalable, chiplet-based architectures — aligned with the goals of the OCP community. These solutions can enable orders of magnitude improvements in bandwidth, density, and flexibility.

### Splitting the Die: A Modular Approach to Chiplet Design and Verification
Speakers: Mark Knight (Arm)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7169/ | materials: https://developer.arm.com/documentation/den0145/latest
AI is accelerating demand for scalable, modular silicon—driving a shift toward multi-die systems built from interoperable chiplets. A viable chiplet marketplace requires standardization that enables chiplets to be reused across systems developed independently by different vendors. This session examines the challenges of adapting monolithic SoCs to modular architectures, with a focus on the design, verification, and system integration standards needed to enable reliable multi-vendor chiplet composition. It also introduces the Arm Chiplet System Architecture, a specification that enables Arm-based systems to be cleanly partitioned across dies using standard interfaces and protocols. The talk will highlight how these foundations support a composable approach to silicon—aligning with the needs of next-generation AI systems and accelerating the creation of an open, reusable chiplet ecosystem.

### Technical Paths to the New Era of GPU-initiated Storage
Speakers: CJ Newburn (NVIDIA), Vikram Sharma Mailthody (NVIDIA)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7069/ | materials: https://www.nvidia.com/en-us/on-demand/session/gtc25-s73012/ | pdf: https://arxiv.org/pdf/2203.04910
We are entering a new era where the need to constrain data-intensive apps to fit into memory is ending. Accessing data in storage vs. memory offers the possibility of much larger data sets, and storage offers TCO advantages. Data-intensive apps process large quantities of data but focus more on data access than computation. GPUs offer significant advantages over CPUs, including 100x more threads and higher memory bandwidth. If only GPUs could access storage directly, rather than relying on CPUs to perform accesses for them! Enter SCADA, for scalable accelerated data access, which allows each of 100,000 threads to concurrently initiate their own requests for data in storage, offers a GPU-side cache to harvest any available locality, and securely performs accesses to storage at rates bounded only by PCIe bandwidth. This talk presents the latest developments for SCADA, motivates this solution, and addresses the latest technical challenges we are navigating with Storage-Next partners.

page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7431/
With the contribution of Caliptra 2.1 with support for OCP LOCK and the ratification of the TCG MEK-MPA feature set, we will discuss why CSPs need Caliptra, LOCK and MEK-MPA support in storage devices to enable Security and allow for circularity once the storage device is decommissioned. Lee Prewitt - Microsoft Jeff Anderson - Google Charles Kunzman - Google

### Deploying and scaling QLC Flash at Meta
Speakers: Madhavan Ravi (Meta), Jesse Koh (Meta)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6804/ | materials: https://engineering.fb.com/2025/03/04/data-center-engineering/a-case-for-qlc-ssds-in-the-data-center/
Driven by the need to deploy power efficient solutions for our Storage infrastructure, we are investing aggressively in deploying QLC Flash based Storage Servers. This talk will 1) give an overview of the Server solution we are starting our deployments with, how we quickly pivoted an existing solution into an OCP Rack (ORv3 based) solution in partnership with a 'new' ODM, 2) how userspace block driver (ublk) brought us flexibility in deploying non-standard drives 3) complications and bottlenecks we keep in mind when designing the QLC based solution and how this will evolve into the future, 4) Meta's aspirations on E2 form factor intercept.

### Sprandom - A fast, pseudo random preconditioning method for high-capacity solid state drives. Plus an update on integration in to FIO.
Speakers: Steven Sprouse (SanDisk)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6738/ | video: https://youtu.be/ppPGAngXX7c?t=5161
As SSD capacities increase beyond 16TB, the time to randomly precondition these drives has also increased from several hours to several days. Traditional methods involve a sequential write followed by multiple random writes to reach a steady state. We present Sprandom (SanDisk Pseudo Random) – a novel approach to random preconditioning that uses the Flexible I/O Tester (fio) to achieve near steady-state performance with just a single physical drive write, reducing preconditioning times for high-capacity drives from days to hours. We will also update the community on our progress with integrating Sprandom in to FIO.

### Advancing OCP Telemetry Standards – Insights from a Drive Vendor
Speakers: Kevin Brandt (Micron Technology)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6936/
OCP standardized telemetry has brought significant advancements in SSD fleet-level health monitoring by establishing a unified approach. In various OCP forums, hyperscalers have shared how these standards have positively impacted their operations. In this talk, Micron aims to provide a perspective as a drive vendor, focusing on: - The journey from proprietary telemetry systems to standardized, human-readable telemetry based on OCP specifications, and the advantages this shift has offered. - Current opportunities and challenges faced with OCP standardized telemetry, including a call to action for industry-wide collaboration to address these obstacles effectively.

### Experiences in Designing a Better NVMe Controller
Speakers: Tim Amundsen (Scaleflux), Matt Bromage (Arm)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7406/
As demand for active self-monitoring increases and performance scales with each generation of PCIe, SSD controllers are being pushed to their limits. CPU architectures commonly found in NVMe SSD SoCs, which rely on cache prefetching, lack the single-cycle memory access required for real-time drive self-monitoring. Introduced in OCP 2.0 and refined in OCP 2.5 and 2.6, features such as latency monitoring and the FDP-enable bit in IO write commands benefit from faster firmware (FW) command fetch enabled by tightly coupled memory (TCM)— available on the Arm R82 architecture. NVMe SoC designers must prepare for a future of increasingly sophisticated self-monitoring drives by adopting architectures that incorporate tightly coupled memory.

### Flash in Fleet
Speakers: Vineet Parekh (Meta)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7293/
- Flash fleet in Sustaining - Deploying features at Scale

### NVMe™ Interoperable Migration of PCIe™ virtual SSDs
Speakers: Michael Allison (Samsung), Lee Prewitt (Microsoft)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/6974/ | video: https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ppPGAngXX7c
This presentation explains the justification and protocol for NVM Express™ providing virtual SSD (i.e., an Exported NVM Subsystem) support in SSDs supporting multiple NVMe™ controllers. This presentation covers the process to create and migrate virtual NVMe SSDs (i.e., Exported NVM Subsystems) that is interoperable between SSD vendors. The commands used by a host to perform these operations are fully illustrated using animation.

### Navigating Shared Storage: Mitigating Performance Challenges with SSD Quality of Service
Speakers: Dan Helmick (Samsung), Yuliya Tarnikova (Google)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7113/
In shared storage environments, maintaining consistent Quality of Service (QoS) is paramount. This presentation delves into the complexities of shared SSD (Solid State Drive) performance, where the actions of one Virtual Machine (VM) can significantly impact others. We'll explore a scenario with multiple VMs sharing an SSD, highlighting how "bad actors" — VMs issuing excessive read, write, or deallocate commands — can disrupt agreed-upon Service Level Agreements (SLAs). The presentation will then investigate how QoS parameters can be effectively implemented and interpreted by SSDs to mitigate these disruptions. Our discussion will cover the intricacies of predicting command interactions and ensuring a more predictable and reliable storage environment for all VMs. Ultimately, we aim to explore practical QoS implementation strategies to meet the stringent expectations of hyperscalers and end-customers.

### Drive Regeneration in Action: Enhancing Fault Tolerance in Datacenters  -
Speakers: Dave Craton (Seagate Technology), Curtis Stevens (Seagate Technology)
page: https://2025ocpglobal.fnvirtual.app/a/schedule/7478/
Datacenter reliability and storage availability are critical to ensuring uninterrupted access to data in today’s fast-paced digital landscape. Today, HDD defects (such as media defects) are managed as part of fault tolerance. Storage Element Depopulation, also known as Drive Regeneration, is an innovative HDD technology that expands fault tolerance by allowing drives with larger defects (such as a degraded recording head) to remain operational rather than being decommissioned. This talk will explore the types of Drive Regeneration, detailing its mechanisms for isolating defective regions while preserving most of the drive’s capacity. Real-world examples and best practices will be shared to illustrate how this technology is being deployed today to optimize storage availability and minimize the need to deploy service personnel for replacements.
